# LORE Crypto Season One — all 100 briefs

Complete draft writing pack · 26 September 2026 · 400 Easter egg concepts

Use the [brief index](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/README.md) for individual files. Review order is not final collector numbering. Eleven approved artworks remain unchanged. This is a brief pack, not 100 approved artworks.

# Brief standard — LORE Crypto Season One

Revision v1, 26 September 2026. These are **100 editorial and illustration briefs with 400 Easter egg concepts**, prepared from selection v1 and the existing approved-card register. This pack is not an artwork approval or a claim that every proposed detail has received a new primary-source check.

## Read a brief

Each brief specifies the historical moment, why it earns a card, the scene, composition, mood, factual limits, four discoverable clues, copy status and source leads. The three-digit prefix is review order only, not final collector numbering. Working titles and the 89 new moment choices remain proposals.

Clue labels distinguish **Historical reference** (a fact or theme with an inherited research lead; its physical prop is still invented), **Symbolic detail** (an artistic interpretation), **Collection crossover** (a deliberate reference to another selected moment), and **Detail requiring targeted verification**. A label does not certify source verification. Each clue has its own meaning and location; four date/number variants of the same fact do not satisfy the intent.

Every concept is a proposed hiding place, except the few details explicitly recorded as retained in an already approved image. The approved register describes those retained details; this text-only pass does not claim to have newly inspected all art. A forward crossover is intentionally anachronistic. Keep that distinction in the event-page explanation and never use it as documentary evidence. If Dan removes a crossover, replace it with a different event-specific clue so the eventual card still has at least four approved clues.

## Scene and clue hierarchy

The historical action must read first. Integrate clues into the scene's drawn surfaces, perspective and lighting; no floating explanation labels. Clue 1 should be discoverable at normal viewing size, clue 2 on a closer look, clue 3 as a subtle visual puzzle, and clue 4 as a collection connection or deep cut. These are relative priorities, not a fixed grid or permission to overcrowd the art.

Keep clues clear of title, phrase, badge, QR and cut-risk regions in the existing art window. Avoid microscopic text. If the four details do not survive a finished-size proof, simplify their shapes or move them within a newly reviewed candidate. Final clue visibility requires inspection of actual artwork; this pack cannot certify it. Do not conceal a fifth functional QR, payment destination, private key or credential as a puzzle.

## Locked LORE treatment

Use LORE-CRYPTO-STYLE-v1.1 across the entire image: confident anime/manhwa contours, expressive faces, grouped hair or fur, two or three cel-shaded tones, cinematic light and equally drawn backgrounds and props. Avoid photoreal food/fur, glossy 3D rendering, airbrush mush and generic grunge. Vary the scene, camera and palette by event; do not import the literal dog, man or room from the references into unrelated scenes.

Before every actual generation or edit, inspect, hash-check and directly supply **both** pinned style images:

- `cards/crypto/season-01/birth-of-hodl-master-02/art.png` — SHA-256 `412f3c10be87a73afbf04c292d5bae605382189f6f34bd925b6cfb77555632d1`.
- `cards/crypto/season-01/birth-of-doge-master-02/art.png` — SHA-256 `6b106345ef9c6564e7ede345196eb34120eb19b5e4a1240af0f33b9e5876f422`.

Event photography, recordings and product images supply factual content references, not a replacement style. For a named person or recognisable historical object, verify likeness, period and geometry before production. Source access does not itself grant image-reproduction rights. These written briefs do not request generation.

Generate illustration separately from brand lettering. Composite the exact approved LORE wordmark/crown and typeset the agreed text afterwards. Preserve LORE-FRONT-v4 and the current print master `LORE-CRYPTO-PRINT-v1.0`: 816×1110 px at 300 DPI, cut 744×1038, safe 684×981, uniform `translate(46.515 48.921) scale(0.8033)`, crypto phrase 25/700. Resolve the shared back from the current approved master; do not redesign it. Brand and layout files remain authoritative.

## Dates, phrases and approvals

Retain the selection's exact date precision and qualifications. Year-only, month-only and multi-day events do not acquire fabricated day dates. Distinguish publication, attack, disclosure, filing, implementation and later outcome. Future cards describing historical laws or rulings must identify their historical scope, not imply their legal position is unchanged today.

For the 11 visually approved cards, preserve the exact art, title, phrase, date, rarity, placement and files. Four clue entries do not mean four new details have been inserted. Optional additions require a separate proposed revision and Dan's visual approval. Existing source-note qualifications continue to apply. Visual approval is separate from phrase verification and print release.

For the other 89 cards, the brief's prose is editorial guidance, **not a final printed moment phrase**. Select a short authentic excerpt from the specific original post, paper, recording or announcement; record exact location/timestamp and a source-backed shortlist for Dan. If no suitable phrase can be verified, disclose that and agree an editorial alternative. Never silently invent a quotation. Do not assign new rarities, collector numbers, edition quantities or foil odds here.

First Transfer and First Halving remain paused. Existing parked Slush Pool and Silk Road concepts remain parked. Writing their briefs does not authorise restarting their artwork. Other parked concepts outside this selected 100, including Dogecar, remain untouched.

## Evidence and economical next steps

Sources and event summaries are inherited from the saved year-shard research and selection v1, with existing uncertainty retained. No exhaustive new source audit or numerical event ranking was performed for this writing pass. The clue explanations are research directions where their details go beyond that source summary. Exact dates, numbers, quotations, products and legal/technical depictions need targeted checks before final copy/art; do not restart research on all 1,229 archive candidates.

Review the chosen moment and four clues, resolve only its specific evidence gaps, approve the brief/copy, then make and review one new card at a time. Artwork, final copy, evidence, QR routing, physical proof and print release are separate statuses. No asset, demo QR, existing print-release flag or approved register is changed by this pack.

# 001 — Blind Signatures

**Date:** 1982  ·  **Era:** Foundations

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0001` → `LORE-EVT-0001` / `E0001`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Chaum presented Blind Signatures for Untraceable Payments at CRYPTO '82, a direct foundation for privacy-preserving electronic cash.

**Why it earns a card:** Introduces the privacy problem digital cash tries to solve.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** An early cryptographer demonstrates private digital cash by signing an opaque envelope while the payment inside stays concealed. The drama is the separation of trust from identity, expressed through a human exchange.

**Composition:** Three-quarter tabletop view; hands and envelope form the focal triangle. Keep faces secondary and the far side of the table in shadow.

**Mood:** Quiet discovery; warm paper against cool terminal light.

**Factual boundaries:** No Bitcoin logos in an ostensibly 1982 room; any later reference must be openly classified as a crossover.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Blind stamp

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** An embossed seal partly hidden beneath the envelope flap.

**Meaning:** Blind signatures let a signer authenticate without seeing the underlying message; the envelope is a metaphor, not a photographed historical prop.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Private payment

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small payment slip whose amount remains visible while its name field is covered.

**Meaning:** Connects the scene to untraceable payments rather than ordinary encrypted correspondence.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Broken footprint trail

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Tiny footprints embossed into the tabletop stop before the recipient's hand.

**Meaning:** A visual puzzle for the intended separation between payment and identity; not a claim that metadata disappears automatically.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. The later paper

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A tiny unlabelled foldout with a connected-node sketch peeks from a drawer.

**Meaning:** A deliberately anachronistic link to The Whitepaper; retain as an optional collection crossover, not a 1982 artifact.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [005 — The Whitepaper](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/005-the-whitepaper.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

## Copy and production handoff

Use the original event post, paper, recording or announcement for a short source-checked shortlist; record its exact location and let Dan agree the line. Brief prose is not a quotation. Rarity and collector number remain unassigned.

Apply the [shared brief standard](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/brief-standard.md): both pinned image references for actual generation, locked brand/layout/print geometry, natural clue placement and finished-size readability. Existing visual masters, QR and print-release states remain unchanged.

## Evidence and open checks

**Retained event flags:** DATE_PRECISION_OR_RANGE

Check exact text/numbers, technical or legal detail, and any recognisable period object before rendering. Symbolic props and crossovers must remain labelled interpretations.

# 002 — The Cypherpunk Manifesto

**Date:** 1993-03-09  ·  **Era:** Foundations

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0004` → `LORE-EVT-0004` / `E0004`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Eric Hughes published A Cypherpunk's Manifesto to the Cypherpunks mailing list.

**Why it earns a card:** Gives the movement a clear human philosophy and voice.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A late-night group shares a newly written privacy manifesto over a mailing-list terminal. Show conviction and practical work: one person types while another passes a sealed note into the room.

**Composition:** Over-shoulder view with a readable human gesture; use a doorway and cables to suggest a wider unseen community.

**Mood:** Defiant, intimate and constructive; amber desk light and deep blue shadows.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not invent a documented meeting, named attendees or a source quotation.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Mailing-list routing

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small branching envelope diagram on the terminal bezel.

**Meaning:** The manifesto circulated through the Cypherpunks mailing list; the bezel doodle is an interpretive hiding place.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Code brackets

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A pair of opening and closing code brackets carved into the keyboard wrist rest.

**Meaning:** Represents the movement's privacy-through-code philosophy without fabricating a spoken slogan.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. A personal curtain

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A narrow desk curtain shields one private note while the public manifesto stays visible.

**Meaning:** Distinguishes choosing privacy from hiding every public act.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Blind-signature seal

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The sealed note carries the envelope motif from Blind Signatures.

**Meaning:** Connects the movement's politics to an earlier digital-cash technique.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [001 — Blind Signatures](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/001-blind-signatures.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 003 — Hashcash

**Date:** 1997-05  ·  **Era:** Foundations

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0005` → `LORE-EVT-0005` / `E0005`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Adam Back's later Hashcash paper states that Hashcash was originally proposed in May 1997 as an anti-spam/proof-of-work mechanism.

**Why it earns a card:** Connects anti-spam proof of work to Bitcoin's ancestry.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A programmer converts repetitive computer work into a small postage-like token, while a flood of unwanted messages slows at the edge of the desk. Make the cost of sending spam understandable through physical action.

**Composition:** Low angle across a desk; one bright stamped message breaks through a darker pile of unwanted mail.

**Mood:** Ingenious and industrious; green terminal glow with restrained copper highlights.

**Factual boundaries:** No claim that Hashcash was a spendable Bitcoin-like currency or required industrial mining rigs.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Anti-spam tray

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small inbox at the edge contains many identical unwanted envelopes.

**Meaning:** Hashcash was proposed as an anti-spam proof-of-work mechanism.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Work before delivery

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A perforated stamp sits beside a completed computation tally.

**Meaning:** The stamp symbolises demonstrable computational work; it is not a literal historical token.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Asymmetric effort

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A worn crank on the sender's side faces a tiny inspection lens on the recipient's side.

**Meaning:** Suggests work to create and easier checking of a proof; keep it a conceptual machine.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. The future block

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The base of the stamp is shaped like one small block from the Genesis composition.

**Meaning:** A collection link to Bitcoin's later use of proof of work, not a Bitcoin object present in 1997.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [006 — Genesis Block](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/006-genesis-block.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 004 — Reusable Proof of Work

**Date:** 2004-08-15  ·  **Era:** Foundations

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0008` → `LORE-EVT-0008` / `E0008`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Hal Finney announced RPOW, a server that exchanged Hashcash proofs for transferable reusable proof-of-work tokens.

**Why it earns a card:** Recognises Hal Finney's pre-Bitcoin contribution.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Hal Finney's reusable-proof-of-work experiment is imagined as a workshop that accepts a used computational proof and returns a transferable token. Emphasise curiosity and careful construction rather than money raining from a machine.

**Composition:** Hands exchange a token across a compact server; an input chute and return path are visible in one frame.

**Mood:** Patient invention; cool silver hardware and warm human skin tones.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not portray RPOW as Bitcoin, a fully decentralised blockchain, or a documented photograph of Finney's actual room.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. The input stamp

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A Hashcash-style work stamp is caught in the input tray.

**Meaning:** RPOW accepted Hashcash proofs as input.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Reusable token track

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small token travels along a curved return channel instead of falling into a waste bin.

**Meaning:** The reusable-token idea is the defining difference in this experiment.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. The server boundary

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The exchange mechanism sits inside a clearly bounded server housing.

**Meaning:** The historical system used a server; avoid implying Bitcoin-style peer-to-peer consensus.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 4. A second terminal

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A dim receiver terminal across the room bears the same ten-dot constellation planned for The First Transfer.

**Meaning:** A forward connection to Finney's later role; not an assertion about the 2004 experiment's payment amount.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [007 — The First Transfer](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/007-the-first-transfer.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 005 — The Whitepaper

**Date:** 2008-10-31  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. Approved art preserved; optional revisions need separate approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0009` → `LORE-EVT-0009` / `E0009`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Satoshi Nakamoto posted Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System to the Cryptography Mailing List on 31 October 2008.

**Why it earns a card:** The public introduction of Bitcoin; existing approved card.

**Approved interpretation to preserve:** Nine illuminated pages become connections above a fictional city. Symbolic interpretation of the whitepaper publication, not a reconstruction of Satoshi, a real city or the January 2009 network launch.

**Approved date / rarity:** 2008-10-31 / legendary.

**Locked artwork:** [cards/crypto/season-01/the-whitepaper-master-01/art.png](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/the-whitepaper-master-01/art.png).

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Reference the exact approved Whitepaper artwork and preserve its focal scene, all wording, rarity and layout. This brief records the story: the Bitcoin proposal enters public discussion. The four clues below are review-only opportunities for a separately approved future revision.

**Composition:** Use the locked composition unchanged; record possible clue locations only after inspecting the actual master.

**Mood:** Preserve the approved lighting and palette.

**Factual boundaries:** No replacement scene, new caption or additional clue may be silently applied to the approved master.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Mailing-list envelope

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Candidate placement: a small paper sleeve or message icon in an existing secondary area.

**Meaning:** The proposal was announced to the Cryptography Mailing List.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Peer-to-peer sketch

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Candidate placement: a modest equal-node doodle in a document margin.

**Meaning:** The design describes payments without a trusted financial intermediary; do not draw a central bank as the network controller.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Hashcash stamp

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Candidate placement: a partly covered stamp at the edge of the document.

**Meaning:** A backward reference to the proof-of-work ancestry; not a claim about a real object on Satoshi's desk.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [003 — Hashcash](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/003-hashcash.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 006 — Genesis Block

**Date:** 2009-01-03T18:15:05Z  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0010` → `LORE-EVT-0010` / `E0010`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Bitcoin's genesis block was timestamped 3 January 2009 and embedded the Times headline about a possible second bank bailout.

**Why it earns a card:** The beginning of Bitcoin's chain and its defining newspaper reference.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A lone first block sits in a vast unfinished network while a newspaper fragment is embedded in its foundation. The scene should feel like the beginning of something improbable, with no visible identity assigned to Satoshi.

**Composition:** Low viewpoint at the first block; faint future block positions recede upward, leaving the main block unmistakable.

**Mood:** Solemn beginning; dawn light breaking through cold architectural shadow.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not identify Satoshi, fabricate a physical mining site or label the embedded headline as a proven statement of personal motive.

**Meaning:** The genesis coinbase contains the Times bailout headline; final legible wording must be taken from the block record.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Meaning:** The block begins the chain; the socket is a visual representation, not a protocol component.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Date in the paper

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The newspaper's small masthead date uses the source-established 3 January 2009.

**Meaning:** Its connection to the embedded newspaper is meaningful, rather than a decorative duplicate of the card date.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

# 007 — The First Transfer

**Date:** 2009-01-12T03:30:25Z  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** PAUSED_BY_USER

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0011` → `LORE-EVT-0011` / `E0011`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Bitcoin block 170 contains the first known person-to-person Bitcoin transaction: 10 BTC sent from Satoshi to Hal Finney.

**Why it earns a card:** Makes the new network a transaction between two people.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Keep this concept in its existing paused state. A possible future scene uses two separate human-scale workstations and a single completed transfer, giving the event warmth without pretending the participants shared a room.

**Composition:** Two distinct pools of light connected by one restrained transaction path; avoid a central handshake or faceless hooded hero.

**Mood:** Tentative success; quiet blue and amber, not explosive wealth.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not restart artwork. Do not invent Satoshi's appearance or claim an in-person meeting.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

**Meaning:** The recorded transfer was 10 BTC; dots avoid treating a token icon as a physical coin.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Block 170

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A compact block label is inset into the transaction path.

**Meaning:** The first known person-to-person transfer is recorded in block 170.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Separate workstations

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Only one chair occupies each of the two distant desk silhouettes.

**Meaning:** This is a network transaction between participants, not evidence of a shared physical setting.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 4. RPOW token

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small reusable-work token from the earlier Finney brief sits near the receiver.

**Meaning:** A backward link to Finney's earlier experiment; not a documented desk object.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [004 — Reusable Proof of Work](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/004-reusable-proof-of-work.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

**Why it earns a card:** A memorable contrast with Bitcoin's later scarcity narrative.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A curious early user claims five BTC from a modest website, with the giveaway expressed through a literal tap only as a visual metaphor. The mood is discovery before prices dominate the story.

**Composition:** A small human reaction and an open browser form the focal point; the tap metaphor stays integrated into the desk rather than becoming a separate diagram.

**Mood:** Generous and playful; clear daylight with restrained blue water reflections.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not show the faucet as a current offer or imply that it created new Bitcoin by itself.

# 010 — The Overflow

**Date:** 2010-08-15  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0013` → `LORE-EVT-0013` / `E0013`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

A value-overflow flaw was exploited in block 74,638 to create roughly 184.47 billion invalid BTC; developers rapidly patched the software and the valid chain rejected the bad history.

**Why it earns a card:** A near-catastrophic bug and rapid repair in Bitcoin's infancy.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A developer catches a numerical overflow before a corrupted wall of blocks overwhelms the room. One valid chain is being repaired while a monstrous false balance dissolves away.

**Composition:** A sharply angled counter and a single corrective action dominate; use the false chain as a looming background, not pages of code.

**Mood:** Urgent problem-solving; sickly overflow light against clean restored blue.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not suggest the invalid coins remain in today's supply or portray the rescue as one person's unsupported solo achievement.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Block 74,638

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small damaged block carries the recorded block height.

**Meaning:** Identifies the specific value-overflow incident.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Impossible balance

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A partly obscured counter reads approximately 184.47 billion.

**Meaning:** The impossible quantity refers to invalid BTC created by the bug, not valid circulation.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Rejected branch

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A broken side-chain of blocks ends in dust behind the repaired path.

**Meaning:** The valid chain rejected the bad history after the fix.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 4. Intact reward gear

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small undamaged reward gear remains in the repaired machinery.

**Meaning:** Foreshadows the programmed supply schedule tested by the first halving; it is an artistic link, not a literal repair part.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [015 — The First Halving](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/015-the-first-halving.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

## The moment

Marek 'Slush' Palatinus announced a cooperative mining pool, widely recognized as the first public Bitcoin mining pool.

**Why it earns a card:** The move from solitary mining toward organised pools.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Retain the parked Slush Pool art state. A future concept could show ordinary miners bringing separate streams of work into one shared mechanism, with small regular payouts replacing a single rare jackpot.

**Composition:** Several modest workstations converge toward one pool; keep one foreground operator as the human focus and avoid a wall of near-identical GPUs.

**Mood:** Cooperative and practical; warm individual lamps feeding cool shared light.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not restart the rejected concept or treat the pool's announcement as proof of the exact first pooled block time.

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 2. Proportional cups

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Meaning:** Represents proportional participation rather than equal rewards regardless of work.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Solo empty tray

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A disconnected workstation has a large empty jackpot tray beside it.

**Meaning:** Explains the variance problem without implying that solo mining could never succeed.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Transport label

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

# 012 — WikiLeaks Accepts Bitcoin

**Date:** 2011-06-14  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

## The moment

WikiLeaks publicly announced that it was accepting anonymous Bitcoin donations after conventional payment channels had been restricted.

**Why it earns a card:** A clear early test of censorship-resistant payments.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A donation reaches a publisher through a narrow open network route while conventional payment doors remain closed. Keep the scene about access to payments and public communication, without reproducing sensitive leaked documents.

**Composition:** A human hand sends a modest donation across the foreground; blocked payment entrances form the background context.

**Mood:** Tense but determined; restrained white light through a dark financial corridor.

**Factual boundaries:** No invented internal WikiLeaks scene, leaked personal material or implication that all donations are untraceable.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Closed payment slots

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Small sealed conventional-payment slots line the background wall.

**Meaning:** Conventional payment channels had been restricted before Bitcoin donations were announced.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Donation receipt

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A plain receipt is marked as a donation rather than a purchase.

**Meaning:** The selected event concerns accepting contributions, not launching a trading business.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Open network thread

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A thin lit cable passes through the remaining open aperture.

**Meaning:** Symbolises censorship resistance; do not represent Bitcoin as immune to every form of restriction.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Two-colour parcel tie

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A tiny blue-and-yellow tie appears only on a secondary parcel.

**Meaning:** An intentional connection to later crypto fundraising for Ukraine, not a 2011 historical object.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

# 013 — Casascius

**Date:** 2011-09-01  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0077` → `LORE-EVT-0077` / `E0077`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Mike Caldwell began producing Casascius physical bitcoins: funded Bitcoin private keys embedded beneath tamper-evident holograms on physical coins.

**Why it earns a card:** A physical collectible that makes digital bearer value tangible.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A collector studies a physical Bitcoin coin and its intact security seal. Make the fascination tactile while keeping the distinction between the metal object and the private key clear.

**Composition:** Macro three-quarter view of fingertips, coin edge and seal; a soft display-case background keeps the silhouette uncluttered.

**Mood:** Tactile wonder; brushed metal and carefully drawn holographic light.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not expose a usable private key or imply that every similar souvenir coin contains funded Bitcoin.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Hologram edge

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A partly lifted but unopened-looking seal edge is visible in a secondary reflection.

**Meaning:** The historical coins hid key material beneath tamper-evident holograms; the sealed main coin stays intact.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Hidden-key recess

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A cutaway shown only in a small technical sketch beneath the case suggests a key compartment.

**Meaning:** The funded private key, not the decorative metal alone, gave the bearer coin its digital value.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Maker's card

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small exhibit label identifies Mike Caldwell as the maker.

**Meaning:** This is a source-backed attribution, with actual marking geometry checked before use.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 4. Future wallet outline

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A reflection in the display glass subtly resembles an early hardware wallet.

**Meaning:** Connects physical key custody to the later Trezor story without treating the two products as equivalent.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [029 — Keys in Your Pocket](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/029-keys-in-your-pocket.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 014 — Litecoin Launches

**Date:** 2011-10-13  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0017` → `LORE-EVT-0017` / `E0017`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Charlie Lee announced Litecoin on 9 October 2011; Litecoin's genesis block was mined about three days later, and the planned public launch time was 13 October.

**Why it earns a card:** An enduring early alternative to Bitcoin.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Two different mining rhythms run beside each other as Litecoin begins its own path. An ordinary workstation and a human operator should ground the silver metaphor in a real software launch.

**Composition:** A foreground operator starts a second machine; the newer lane diverges gently rather than destroying Bitcoin's lane.

**Mood:** Fresh and optimistic; silver-grey with warm workshop light.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not portray Litecoin as a Bitcoin upgrade, declare a rivalry winner or collapse announcement, genesis and public launch into one timestamp.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Three-stage calendar

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Three unobtrusive calendar tabs distinguish announcement, genesis and planned public launch.

**Meaning:** The saved research describes a multi-stage October launch; final labels need the source dates.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Scrypt work tile

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small tile marked Scrypt rests beside the new machine.

**Meaning:** Litecoin used a different proof-of-work function from Bitcoin.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Silver companion

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small silver-coloured tool lies alongside an older gold-coloured tool.

**Meaning:** An interpretation of the digital-silver narrative, not precious-metal backing.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Unequal metronomes

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A tiny clockwork rhythm motif points toward later block-reward stories.

**Meaning:** A collection link to recurring network time; do not turn it into an unsourced exact timing claim.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [093 — The Fourth Halving](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/093-the-fourth-halving.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 015 — The First Halving

**Date:** 2012-11-28  ·  **Era:** Bitcoin begins

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** PAUSED_BY_USER

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0018` → `LORE-EVT-0018` / `E0018`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

At block 210,000, Bitcoin's block subsidy fell from 50 BTC to 25 BTC.

**Why it earns a card:** Introduces Bitcoin's recurring supply milestone.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Keep the existing art pause. A possible future scene shows miners watching one reward divide cleanly into two, with the machine continuing to run normally. The drama is the rule executing as expected.

**Composition:** Reward mechanism at the centre; human faces react in the lower third; leave the upper art zone clear.

**Mood:** Ritual and anticipation; warm reward light against cool industrial shadow.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not resume artwork or imply that existing wallet balances were halved.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Block 210,000

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small counter on the mechanism lands on the recorded height.

**Meaning:** This is the first halving's activation block.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. 50 to 25

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Two adjacent reward trays show 50 before and 25 after.

**Meaning:** The change applies to the block subsidy, not every holder's balance.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Unbroken belt

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The machine's belt continues past the dividing blade without stopping.

**Meaning:** Represents programmed continuity instead of a network shutdown.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Fourth notch

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A barely visible fourth notch sits farther along the reward wheel.

**Meaning:** Foreshadows the fourth halving as a series motif; it is an illustration of the schedule, not a real machine.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [093 — The Fourth Halving](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/093-the-fourth-halving.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 016 — Silk Road Shutdown

**Date:** 2013-10-01  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** PREVIOUS_ART_PARKED

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0083` → `LORE-EVT-0083` / `E0083`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Federal agents arrested Ross Ulbricht in a San Francisco library and seized his laptop; the Silk Road site was shut down in the same enforcement operation.

**Why it earns a card:** A defining collision between Bitcoin use and law enforcement.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Keep the rejected Silk Road illustration parked. A fresh future brief would focus on the abrupt interruption of a library work session, preserving the tension around an open laptop without a generic action-movie raid.

**Composition:** Close, human-scale library setting; the laptop and interrupted hands are the focal action, with shelves framing depth.

**Mood:** Quiet tension breaking suddenly; neutral library light with controlled contrast.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not restart art. Do not put the later 144,336-BTC seizure total on the arrest-day screen or invent exact screen contents.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Library classification

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small generic library spine label sits beside the laptop.

**Meaning:** The arrest took place in a San Francisco library; shelf contents are invented scenery, not evidence.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Open session

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small awake-status light remains visible on the laptop.

**Meaning:** The seized laptop is central to the recorded arrest; its exact display must come from a reference before rendering.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. A severed route

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A thin silk-like thread trails from the desk and stops at a closed door.

**Meaning:** Symbolises the marketplace shutdown without glamorising the trade.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. The distant gate

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small reflection of an open gate hides in the laptop lid.

**Meaning:** An explicitly later-story reference to the 2025 pardon, not a prediction attributed to the people in the scene.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [097 — The Pardon](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/097-the-pardon.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

**Why it earns a card:** The first permanent public Bitcoin ATM gives a strong real-world setting.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A customer exchanges cash and Bitcoin at the Robocoin kiosk inside Waves Coffee House. The strongest story is the ordinary cafe around an extraordinary new machine.

**Composition:** Three-quarter view that keeps both kiosk and customer hands believable; a coffee cup provides scale and a shop window adds depth.

**Mood:** Curious and welcoming; rainy Vancouver light outside, warm cafe light within.

**Factual boundaries:** Verify kiosk geometry from contemporary photographs. Do not depict a one-way voucher dispenser or put an impossible perspective on the screen.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Vancouver rain map

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Why it earns a card:** Captures Bitcoin's first widely recognised four-figure price milestone.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** An early exchange user notices Bitcoin crossing four figures on a modest monitor. Keep the reaction small and believable: surprise rather than a room already full of wealth.

**Composition:** Monitor angled correctly within a domestic trading setup; a foreground hand pauses above the keyboard.

**Mood:** Astonishment; screen light against a quiet room.

**Factual boundaries:** Use the recorded venue and milestone, not a universal claim that every exchange crossed at the same second.

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

# 019 — Ethereum's Whitepaper

**Date:** 2013-11-27  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0021` → `LORE-EVT-0021` / `E0021`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Ethereum's official history dates the introductory whitepaper to 27 November 2013; Vitalik Buterin had circulated the first draft to prospective collaborators that month.

**Why it earns a card:** Introduces the broader programmable-blockchain ambition.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A young developer's design paper unfolds into many possible applications rather than only a payment route. Treat the room as an interpreted writing scene and the author as source-referenced, not a photographic reconstruction.

**Composition:** Paper and hands in the foreground; a small branching model of applications rises behind, with a clear single focal direction.

**Mood:** Expansive curiosity; pale violet and warm desk light.

# 022 — Buried Fortune

**Date:** 2013  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. Approved art preserved; optional revisions need separate approval.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0184` → `LORE-EVT-0184` / `E0184`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

In 2013, a discarded hard drive left James Howells unable to access a large Bitcoin holding. His efforts to recover it from a Newport landfill became widely reported in November; the exact disposal date is not established here.

**Why it earns a card:** An enduring human story about losing access to digital wealth.

**Date qualification:** Approved card is year-only. 27 November is a publicity milestone, not a verified date of disposal. No exact disposal day is invented.

**Approved interpretation to preserve:** Poetic landfill cutaway with relative foreground enlargement; not a recovered object or witnessed excavation. Rain, character, machinery, in-scene sign and 2009 tape placement are editorial illustration. A dirty, mostly concealed Doge plush is a fictional visual callback. No value headline appears on the front.

**Approved date / rarity:** 2013 / rare.

**Locked artwork:** [cards/crypto/season-01/buried-fortune-master-01/art.png](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/buried-fortune-master-01/art.png).

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Preserve the approved poetic landfill cutaway, enlarged discarded drive and searching figure. The story is access to a fortune becoming physically unreachable; this is a symbolic view beneath rubbish, not a documented excavation.

**Composition:** Keep the locked scale contrast and existing foreground drive treatment.

**Mood:** Retain rain, industrial gloom and the small human silhouette.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not show recovered coins, a recovered drive, an exact disposal day, or reinstate the removed newspaper and value headline.

**Why it earns a card:** Community fundraising creates a memorable sporting crossover.

**Mood:** Joyful collective effort; tropical accents against cold blue ice.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not imply Dogecoin paid every team expense or determined the Olympic result; avoid invented athlete portraits.

# 024 — Mt. Gox

**Date:** 2014-02-28  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. Approved art preserved; optional revisions need separate approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0022` → `LORE-EVT-0022` / `E0022`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Mt. Gox went offline on 25 February 2014 after halting Bitcoin withdrawals earlier in the month and filed for bankruptcy protection in Tokyo on 28 February.

**Why it earns a card:** The emblematic early exchange collapse; existing approved card.

**Date qualification:** Narrowed from the source collapse range to the bankruptcy date represented on the approved card.

**Approved interpretation to preserve:** Fictional customer outside an imagined Tokyo exchange. Chained glass doors and PENDING display symbolise lost access. Newspaper gives the INITIAL reported 850,000 BTC missing; approximately 200,000 BTC were later located. The address comes from the announcement. The date marks the civil rehabilitation filing, not a one-day theft.

**Approved date / rarity:** 2014-02-28 / epic.

**Locked artwork:** [cards/crypto/season-01/mt-gox-master-01/art.png](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/mt-gox-master-01/art.png).

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Preserve the approved Locked Out scene: a fictional customer outside an imagined Tokyo exchange, chained glass doors and a pending withdrawal. The emotional centre is helpless waiting as an institution fails.

**Composition:** Retain the approved rain, reflections and locked doors; new clues require explicit revision approval.

**Mood:** Preserve the existing rainy palette.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not depict a single-day theft on the bankruptcy date, assert a real building likeness, or treat initial missing and later recovered amounts as interchangeable.

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 2. Broken exchange board

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

### 3. Unopened queue ticket

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Meaning:** A metaphor for creditors waiting; the queue and ticket are fictional.

# 025 — Monero Launches

**Date:** 2014-04-18  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0209` → `LORE-EVT-0209` / `E0209`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

The Monero blockchain went live on 18 April 2014 as a fair-launched CryptoNote-derived privacy cryptocurrency.

**Why it earns a card:** Makes privacy a central thread of the collection.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A sender and recipient exchange value through a crowded night market while identifying trails dissolve among many possible routes. Privacy should feel like ordinary people gaining personal space, without implying criminality.

**Composition:** Two figures separated by layered awnings; the eye can follow several plausible paths but no single glowing surveillance line.

**Mood:** Quiet confidence; copper light and muted charcoal.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not attribute later privacy upgrades to launch day or promise complete anonymity in every circumstance.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Unmarked goods

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A crate of visually interchangeable tokens under a stall counter.

**Meaning:** A metaphor for fungibility, one of the project's central aims.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. CryptoNote notebook

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A closed technical notebook with a small approved text label on its spine.

**Meaning:** Links the launch to its CryptoNote foundations without turning the composition into a technical diagram.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Ambiguous footprints

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Several overlapping footprint trails fade under a rug.

**Meaning:** Represents concealment among possible paths, not a literal protocol trace or guarantee.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Folded privacy curtain

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A curtain corner carries the later Tornado card's spiral pattern.

**Meaning:** A deliberate thematic crossover to the later debate over privacy tools; not an organisational connection.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [082 — Tornado Cash Sanctioned](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/082-tornado-cash-sanctioned.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

## The moment

Ethereum launched its 42-day Ether genesis sale on 22 July 2014, accepting Bitcoin and initially offering 2,000 ETH per BTC.

**Why it earns a card:** The funding moment behind Ethereum's launch.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A digital crowdsale desk exchanges bitcoin contributions for promises of ether in a network still under construction. The distant architecture is unfinished: buyers are backing a future platform.

**Composition:** Foreground exchange counter with a scaffolded computational city beyond; leave the future visibly incomplete.

**Mood:** Hopeful construction; indigo night with amber work lights.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not depict live Ethereum applications in July 2014 or imply the initial sale rate lasted through the whole sale.

### 3. Unlit sockets

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

**Why it earns a card:** Tether's origins help explain the later stablecoin economy.

**Mood:** Practical invention with an undercurrent of trust; teal and cream.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not portray audited one-to-one reserves as independently established by this brief, use a modern chain for the initial launch, or conflate the October launch with the later rename.

# 031 — BitLicense

**Date:** 2015-06-24  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0281` → `LORE-EVT-0281` / `E0281`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

New York's Department of Financial Services made its virtual-currency regulation, commonly called the BitLicense, effective on 24 June 2015.

**Why it earns a card:** A major early attempt to license crypto businesses.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A New York street of emerging crypto businesses meets a newly installed licensing gate. Some operators approach with folders while others study routes around it, making the trade-off visible without assigning villains.

**Composition:** Street-level view with the gate offset; multiple small businesses remain legible beyond it.

**Mood:** Institutional pressure; limestone grey with pockets of startup colour.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not imply the rule covered every global user, invent closures of specific firms, or depict it as a federal law.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. State outline seal

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small New York outline embossed on the application folder.

**Meaning:** Locates the regulation at state level rather than nationwide.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Licence desk

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A document tray marked for virtual-currency business applications.

**Meaning:** Explains the subject of the framework without pretending this is an actual government office.

# 032 — Ethereum Goes Live

**Date:** 2015-07-30T15:26:13Z  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0026` → `LORE-EVT-0026` / `E0026`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Ethereum's Frontier genesis launched on 30 July 2015, bringing the first live Ethereum mainnet into operation.

**Why it earns a card:** Programmable contracts move from plan to functioning mainnet.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** The Ethereum network comes alive like a young city receiving its first power. Builders watch an illuminated chain reach unfinished workspaces, showing the beginning of a platform rather than its mature ecosystem.

**Composition:** Wide upward view; one initial block ignites a branching path through scaffolding.

**Mood:** Dawn, possibility and visible rough edges.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not put later tokens, DAOs or NFT hits into launch-day use; any collection reference must be labelled a flash-forward.

### 2. Executable plan

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Meaning:** Expresses general-purpose on-chain programs; it is symbolic rather than real code.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Incomplete skyline

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Several structures remain scaffolded beside the newly lit route.

**Meaning:** The launch opened a working but early network, not a finished city.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Crowdsale receipt

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

# 033 — The DAO Hack

**Date:** 2016-06-17  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. Approved art preserved; optional revisions need separate approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0028` → `LORE-EVT-0028` / `E0028`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

An attacker exploited a recursive-call/reentrancy vulnerability in The DAO and began draining Ether into a child DAO.

**Why it earns a card:** A foundational smart-contract failure; existing approved card.

**Approved interpretation to preserve:** Fictional sealed vaults visualise The DAO exploit. A one-way violet stream depicts ETH diverted into the locked child DAO. A small amber loop at the SPLIT control represents recursive calls, not funds returned to The DAO. Enlarged fictional engineers convey urgency. The receiver display references the approximate 27-day withdrawal restriction reported during the attack. No actual physical building, identifiable attacker, Ethereum consensus breach or later July fork is depicted.

**Approved date / rarity:** 2016-06-17 / epic.

**Locked artwork:** [cards/crypto/season-01/the-dao-hack-master-01/art.png](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/the-dao-hack-master-01/art.png).

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Preserve the approved DAO vault scene: a one-way violet ETH stream flows into a locked child-DAO vault while fictional engineers react. The amber loop at the SPLIT control represents recursive calls, not funds flowing back.

**Composition:** Retain the approved stream direction, sealed recipient and control-panel hierarchy.

**Mood:** Preserve the approved urgency and contrasting violet and amber.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not show immediately spendable stolen cash, identify a fictional engineer as the attacker, or conflate the exploit with the later chain split.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Nested call tabs

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Candidate placement: small repeating tabs around the existing SPLIT control.

**Meaning:** References reentrant execution; technical design must not reverse the approved flow direction.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Locked withdrawal dial

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Candidate placement: a small restricted-access dial on the recipient vault.

**Meaning:** References the withdrawal waiting period; any duration shown needs exact source wording.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Emergency patch spool

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Candidate placement: an unused repair spool near an engineer's foot.

**Meaning:** Symbolises the scramble to respond without suggesting a single physical fix solved the dispute.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Forked shadow

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Candidate placement: a faint two-branch shadow beyond the vault corridor.

**Meaning:** Foreshadows the later fork; it must not imply that the chains had already split on the exploit date.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [034 — The Split](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/034-the-split.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 034 — The Split

**Date:** 2016-07-20  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0029` → `LORE-EVT-0029` / `E0029`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

At block 1,920,000, the Ethereum hard fork moved DAO-related Ether to a refund contract; miners opposed to the intervention continued the original chain, which became Ethereum Classic.

**Why it earns a card:** The Ethereum/Ethereum Classic fork embodies a lasting governance question.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A single Ethereum railway reaches a contested junction and continues along two illuminated tracks. Builders stand on both sides with competing plans; the scene should convey a difficult community choice without assigning moral superiority.

**Composition:** Central junction viewed from above; both routes retain visual weight and continue beyond frame.

**Mood:** Sombre resolve; related violet palettes diverge subtly.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not depict two different chains before the fork, a unanimous decision, or an erasure of the original chain.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Shared sleepers

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The two tracks share identical sleepers before the junction.

**Meaning:** Both histories coincide before the split.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Refund platform

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** One branch passes a small contract-shaped platform holding return envelopes.

**Meaning:** References the fork's recovery mechanism; the other branch remains visibly continuous.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Two worn maps

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Different route maps lie open on opposite sides of the junction.

**Meaning:** Represents competing views about immutability and intervention rather than a technical protocol map.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Sealed vault freight

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small closed vault appears on a background trolley.

**Meaning:** Links the governance crisis to The DAO exploit without suggesting the vault itself caused consensus to fail.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [033 — The DAO Hack](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/033-the-dao-hack.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 035 — The Bitfinex Heist

**Date:** 2016-08-02  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0095` → `LORE-EVT-0095` / `E0095`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Bitfinex suffered a security breach on 2 August 2016 in which 119,755 BTC were stolen.

**Why it earns a card:** A major custody failure with a later recovery story.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** A trading desk faces a breached reserve vault while customers see balances suddenly become uncertain. Show the human shock and exchange-level failure, with Bitcoin's wider network continuing in the distance.

**Composition:** Broken vault edge in foreground, a worried customer reflected in a dim trading screen.

**Mood:** Cold shock; emergency red used sparingly.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not portray the Bitcoin protocol as hacked, turn later recovery into a same-day event, or render real credentials or a working exploit.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Withdrawal queue

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A stalled queue of small withdrawal slips accumulates beneath a screen.

**Meaning:** References the exchange disruption and customers' loss of access.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. BFX envelope

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A discreet envelope bears the later compensation-token label.

**Meaning:** A labelled aftermath detail about the loss allocation response; not necessarily present at the moment of the hack.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Unequal reflection

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The vault's reflection is incomplete while the distant network lights stay on.

**Meaning:** Separates loss at an intermediary from the continuing underlying network.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Long wait clock

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A clock with an extra slowly moving ring sits behind the desk.

**Meaning:** A thematic crossover to Mt. Gox repayments, not a claim that their legal processes or outcomes matched.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.

**Collection link:** [094 — Mt. Gox Repayments](https://github.com/d4np4yn3-netizen/Lore/blob/main/cards/crypto/season-01/briefs/v1/094-mt-gox-repayments.md). Intentional crossover; a forward reference is anachronistic.

# 037 — The Zcash Ceremony

**Date:** 2016-10-22 to 2016-10-23  ·  **Era:** Crypto expands

**Brief:** Draft for Dan’s review. New moment/scene proposal; no artwork approval.

**Production hold:** No hold recorded in selection v1; artwork still requires its normal review.

**Record:** `BRIEF-E0214` → `LORE-EVT-0214` / `E0214`. Review order is not collector numbering.

## The moment

Six participants generated the Sprout zk-SNARK public parameters through a multi-party trusted-setup ceremony shortly before Zcash launched.

**Why it earns a card:** An unusually tangible ritual behind cryptographic privacy.

## Illustration brief

**Scene:** Six separated workstations contribute pieces to a delicate cryptographic construction while each destroys a private remnant. The scene uses a split-room composition to avoid inventing a single shared ceremony room.

**Composition:** One central public structure surrounded by six visually separate compartments; discarded remnants stay secondary.

**Mood:** Ritual tension with laboratory restraint.

**Factual boundaries:** Do not publish secrets, imply all participants met in one room, or describe the ceremony as eliminating every form of trust or risk.

## Four Easter eggs

Locations are illustration concepts unless explicitly marked as retained. Meaning and historical status must remain clear in the artist handoff.

### 1. Six isolated lamps

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** Each compartment contains one small lamp illuminating its contribution.

**Meaning:** References the six participants in the Sprout setup ceremony.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 2. Shredded remnant

**Type:** Historical reference. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A small shredded strip lies in one compartment beyond the public construction.

**Meaning:** Symbolises secret destruction; the security condition depended on at least one participant keeping and destroying its secret appropriately.

**Evidence:** Inherited event-research lead. Check the specific claim against the listed sources before final text/art; placement is illustrative.

### 3. Unjoinable scraps

**Type:** Symbolic detail. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** The foreground scraps deliberately fail to form a readable message.

**Meaning:** Suggests discarded private material; no actual secret or reconstruction puzzle should be encoded.

**Evidence:** Creative interpretation; no claim that this prop historically existed.

### 4. Privacy market tile

**Type:** Collection crossover. **Status:** CONCEPT_FOR_REVIEW.

**Where:** A tiny tile repeats the anonymous market awning pattern from the Monero brief.

**Meaning:** A thematic connection between different privacy approaches, not a claim the systems share the same cryptography.

**Evidence:** Intentional collection reference, not an object documented at the event.
